The information loss of a stochastic map
James Fullwood, Arthur J. Parzygnat

Abstract
We provide a stochastic extension of the Baez–Fritz–Leinster characterization of the Shannon information loss associated with a measure-preserving function. This recovers the conditional entropy and a closely related information-theoretic measure that we call conditional information loss . Although not functorial, these information measures are semi-functorial, a concept we introduce that is definable in any Markov category. We also introduce the notion of an entropic Bayes’ rule for information measures, and we provide a characterization of conditional entropy in terms of this rule. † † 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 94A17; Secondary 18A05, 62F15 † † Key words and phrases. Bayes; conditional probability; disintegration; entropy; error correction; functor; information theory; Markov category; stochastic map; synthetic probability



1
Introduction

The information loss
K(f)
associated with a measure-preserving function
(X,p)\xrightarrow{f}(Y,q)
between finite probability spaces is given by the Shannon entropy difference

K(f):=H(p)-H(q),

where
H(p):=-\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}\log p_{x}
is the Shannon entropy of
p
(and similarly for
q
).
In
[
1
]
, Baez, Fritz, and Leinster proved that the information loss satisfies, and is uniquely characterized up to a multiplicative factor by, the following conditions:

0.

Positivity
:
K(f)\geq 0
for all
(X,p)\xrightarrow{f}(Y,q)
. This says that the information loss associated with a deterministic process is always non-negative.

1.

Functoriality
:
K(g\circ f)=K(g)+K(f)
for every composable pair
(f,g)
of measure-preserving maps. This says that the information loss of two successive processes is the sum of the information losses associated with each process.

2.

Convex Linearity
:
K(\lambda f\oplus(1-\lambda)g)=\lambda K(f)+(1-\lambda)K(g)
for all
\lambda\in(0,1)
. This says that the information loss associated with tossing a (possibly unfair) coin in deciding amongst two processes is the associated weighted sum of their information losses.

3.

Continuity
:
K(f)
is a continuous function of
f
. This says that the information loss does not change much under small perturbations (i.e., is robust with respect to errors).

As measure-preserving functions may be viewed as deterministic stochastic maps, it is natural to ask whether there exist extensions of the Baez–Fritz–Leinster (BFL) characterization of information loss to maps that are inherently random (i.e., stochastic) in nature. In particular, what information-theoretic quantity captures such an information loss in this larger category?

This question is answered in the present work. Namely, we extend the BFL characterization theorem, which is valid on
deterministic
maps, to the larger category of
stochastic
maps. In doing so, we also find a characterization of the conditional entropy. Although the resulting extension is not functorial on the larger category of stochastic maps, we formalize a weakening of functoriality that restricts to functoriality on deterministic maps. This weaker notion of functoriality is definable in any Markov category
[
3
,
6
]
, and it provides a key axiom in our characterization.

To explain how we arrive at our characterization, let us first recall the definition of stochastic maps between finite probability spaces, for which the measure-preserving functions are a special case. A
stochastic map

associates with every

a probability distribution
f_{x}
on
Y
such that
q_{y}=\sum_{x\in X}f_{yx}p_{x}
, where
f_{yx}
is the distribution
f_{x}
evaluated at
y\in Y
. In terms of information flow, the space
(X,p)
may be thought of as a probability distribution on the set of inputs for a communication channel described by the stochastic matrix
f_{yx}
, while
(Y,q)
is then thought of as the induced distribution on the set of outputs of the channel.

to any

is in fact deterministic, i.e., if

is a point-mass distribution for all

, then

for all

. As such,

is a measure of the uncertainty (or randomness) of the outputs of

averaged over the prior distribution
p
on the set
X
of its inputs. Indeed,

is maximized precisely when
f_{x}
is the uniform distribution on
Y
for all
x\in X
.

2

The same letter
K
is used here because it agrees with the Shannon entropy difference when
f
is deterministic.

As
H(f|p)=0
whenever
f
is deterministic, the conditional information loss restricts to the category of measure preserving functions as the information loss functor of Baez, Fritz, and Leinster, while also satisfying conditions 0, 2, and 3 (i.e., positivity, convex linearity, and continuity) on the larger category of stochastic maps. However, conditional information loss is
not
functorial in general, and while this may seem like a defect at first glance, we prove that there is no extension of the information loss functor that remains functorial on the larger category of stochastic maps if the positivity axiom is to be preserved, thus retaining an interpretation as information loss. In spite of this, conditional information loss does satisfy a weakened form of functoriality, which we briefly describe now.

of composable stochastic maps is
a.e. coalescable
if and only if for every pair of elements
z\in Z
and
x\in X
for which
r_{z}>0
and
p_{x}>0
, there exists a unique
y\in Y
such that
f_{yx}>0
and
g_{zy}>0
. Intuitively, this says that the information about the intermediate step can be recovered given knowledge about the input and output. In particular, if
f
is deterministic, then the pair
(f,g)
is a.e. colescable (for obvious reasons, since knowing
x
alone is enough to determine the intermediate value). However, there are other many situations where a pair could be a.e. coalescable and the maps need not be deterministic. With this definition in place (which we also generalize to the setting of arbitrary Markov categories), we replace functoriality with the following weaker condition.

of stochastic maps. This says that the conditional information loss of two successive processes is the sum of the conditional information losses associated with each process
provided
that the information in the intermediate step can always be recovered.

Replacing functoriality with semi-functoriality is not enough to characterize the conditional information loss. However, it comes quite close, as only one more axiom is needed. Assuming positivity, semi-functoriality, convex linearity, and continuity, there are several equivalent axioms that may be stipulated to characterize the conditional information loss. To explain the first option, we introduce a convenient factorization of every stochastic map
(X,p)\mathrel{\hbox to15.35pt{\vbox to12.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.67252pt\lower-7.71387pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

. A visualization of the bloom of

is given in Figure

. When one is given the additional data of probability measures

and

and
Y
, respectively, then Figure

illustrates the bloom-shriek factorization of
f
. From this point of view,
\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f}
keeps track of the information encoded in
both

p
and
f
, while the projection map
\pi_{Y}
forgets, or loses,
some
of this information.

Figure 1
:
A visualization of bloom and the bloom-shriek factorization via water droplets as inspired by Gromov
[
8
]
. The bloom of
f
splits each water droplet of volume 1 (an element of
X
) into several water droplets whose total volume equates to 1. If
X
has a probability
p
on it, then the initial volume of that water droplet is scaled by this probability. The stochastic map therefore splits the water droplet using this scale.

With this in mind, our final axiom to characterize the conditional information loss is

4 (a).

Reduction
:
K(f)=K(\pi_{Y})
, where
f=\pi_{Y}\circ\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f}
is the bloom-shriek factorization of
f
. This says that the conditional information loss of
f
equals the information loss of the projection using the associated joint distribution on
X\times Y
.

Note that this axiom describes how
K
is determined by its action on an associated class of deterministic morphisms.
These slightly modified axioms, namely, semi-functoriality, convex linearity, continuity, and reduction, characterize the conditional information loss and therefore extend Baez, Fritz, and Leinster’s characterization of information loss. A much simpler axiom that may be invoked in place of the reduction axiom which also characterizes conditional information loss is the following.

4 (b).

Blooming
:
K(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p})=0
, where
\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p}
is the unique map
(\bullet,1)\mathrel{\hbox to10.66pt{\vbox to6.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.33047pt\lower-4.60277pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

from a one point probability space to

. This says that if a process begins with no prior information, then there is no information to be lost in the process.

The conditional entropy itself can be extracted from the conditional information loss by a process known as Bayesian inversion, which we now briefly recall. Given a stochastic map
(X,p)\mathrel{\hbox to15.35pt{\vbox to12.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.67252pt\lower-7.71387pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

as the unique assignment

1^{\star}
, 2, and 3 above, but with a new axiom
that reads as follows.

4 (c).

. This is an information theoretic analogue of Bayes’ rule, which reads
f_{yx}p_{x}=\overline{f}_{xy}q_{y}
for all
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, or in more traditional probabilistic notation
\mathbb{P}(y|x)\mathbb{P}(x)=\mathbb{P}(x|y)\mathbb{P}(y).

In other words, we obtain a
Bayesian
characterization of the conditional entropy.
This provides an entropic and information-theoretic description of Bayes’ rule from the Markov category perspective, in a way that we interpret as answering an open question of Fritz
[
7
]
.

2
Categories of stochastic maps

In the first few sections, we define all the concepts involved in proving that the conditional information loss satisfies the properties that we will later prove characterize it. This section introduces the domain category and its convex structure.

.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
X
be a finite set. The
copy
of
X
is the diagonal embedding
\Delta_{X}:X\to X\times X
, and the
discard
of
X
is the unique map from
X
to the terminal object
\bullet
in
\mathbf{FinStoch}
, which will be denoted by
\operatorname{!}_{X}:X\to\bullet
. If
Y
is another finite set, the
swap map
is the map
\gamma:X\times Y\to Y\times X
given by
(x,y)\mapsto(y,x)
.

The product of stochastic maps endows
\mathbf{FinStoch}
with the structure of a monoidal category. Together with the copy, discard, and swap maps,
\mathbf{FinStoch}
is a Markov category

,

]
.

Lemma 2.8
.

The object
(\bullet,1)
given by a single element set equipped with the unique probability measure is a zero object (i.e. terminal and initial) in
\mathbf{FinPS}
.

0&\mbox{otherwise.}\\
\end{cases}

3
The Baez–Fritz–Leinster characterization of information loss

In
[
1
]
, Baez, Fritz, and Leinster (BFL) characterized the Shannon entropy difference associated with measure-preserving functions between finite probability spaces as the only non-vanishing, continuous, convex linear functor from
\mathbf{FinPD}
to the non-negative reals (up to a multiplicative constant). It is then natural to ask whether there exist either extensions or analogues of their result by including non-deterministic morphisms from the larger category
\mathbf{FinPS}
. Before delving deeper into such inquiry, we first recall in detail the characterization theorem of BFL.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
be the convex category consisting of a single object and whose set of morphisms is
{{\mathbb{R}}}
. The composition in
{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
is given by addition. Convex combinations of morphisms are given by ordinary convex combinations of numbers. The subcategory of non-negative reals will be denoted
{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
.

Convention 3.2
.

In the rest of the paper, we will not necessarily assume that assignments from one category to another are functors. Nevertheless, we do assume they form (class) functions (see
[
10
, Section I.7]
for more details). Furthermore, we assume that they respect or reflect source and targets in the following sense. If
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are two categories, all functions
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
are either
covariant
or
contravariant
in the sense that for any morphism
a\xrightarrow{\gamma}b
in
\mathcal{C}
,
F(\gamma)
is a morphism from
F(a)
to
F(b)
or from
F(b)
to
F(a)
, respectively. These are the only types of functions between categories we will consider in this work. As such, we therefore abuse terminology and use the term
functions
for such assignments throughout. If
M
is a
commutative
monoid and
{{\mathbb{B}}}M
denotes its one object category, then every covariant function
\mathcal{C}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}M
is also contravariant and vice-versa.

We now define a notion of continuity for functions of the form
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
.

and

for all

.

The following limits
5
5

5

Note that these limits necessarily imply
\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}q_{n}=q
.

hold:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}p_{n}=p

and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}=f
.

A function
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
is
continuous
if and only if

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}F(f_{n})=F(f)

whenever
\{f_{n}\}
is a sequence in
\mathbf{FinPS}
converging to
f
.

Remark 3.4
.

In the subcategory
\mathbf{FinPD}
, since the topology of the collection of
functions
from a finite set
X
to another finite set
Y
is
discrete
, one can equivalently assume that a sequence
f_{n}
as in Definition
3.3
, but this time with all
f_{n}
deterministic, converges to
(X,p)\xrightarrow{f}(Y,q)
if and only if the following two conditions hold.

(a)

There exists an
N\in{{\mathbb{N}}}
for which
X_{n}=X,Y_{n}=Y,

f_{n}=f
for all
n\geq N
.

(b)

For
n\geq N
, one has

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}p_{n}=p
.

In this way, our definition of convergence agrees with the definition of convergence of BFL on the subcategory
\mathbf{FinPD}

[
1
]
.

A function

if and only if for all objects

Definition 3.6
.

A function
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
is said to be
functorial
if and only if it is in fact a functor, i.e., if and only if
F(g\circ f)=F(f)+F(g)
for every composable pair
(f,g)
in
\mathbf{FinPS}
.

.

be a probability measure. The
Shannon entropy
of
p
is given by

H(p):=-\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}\log(p_{x}).

Convention 3.8
.

When considering any entropic quantity, we will always adhere to the convention that
0\log(0)=0
.

Definition 3.9
.

Given a map
(X,p)\xrightarrow{f}(Y,p)
in
\mathbf{FinPD}
, the Shannon entropy difference
H(p)-H(q)
will be referred to as the
information loss
of
f
. Information loss defines a functor
K:\mathbf{FinPD}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
, henceforth referred to as the
information loss functor
on
\mathbf{FinPD}
.

Theorem 3.10

(Baez–Fritz–Leinster
[
1
]
)
.

Suppose
F:\mathbf{FinPD}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
is a function which satisfies the following conditions.

1.

F
is functorial.

2.

F
is convex linear.

3.

F
is continuous.

Then
F
is a non-negative multiple of information loss. Conversely, the information loss functor is non-negative and satisfies conditions
1
-
3
.

In light of Theorem
3.10
, it is natural to question whether or not there exists a functor
K:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
that restricts to
\mathbf{FinPD}
as the information loss functor. It turns out that no such non-vanishing functor exists, as we prove in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.11
.

If
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
is a functor, then
F(f)=0
for all morphisms
f
in
\mathbf{FinPS}
.

Proof.

Let
(X,p)\mathrel{\hbox to15.35pt{\vbox to12.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.67252pt\lower-7.71387pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.11975pt}{-1.75pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{}{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}

be a morphism in

. Since

is a functor,

F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{q})=F(f\circ\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p})=F(f)+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p})\implies 0\leq F(f)=F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{q})-F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p}).

(which necessarily exists by Example

, for instance). Then a similar calculation yields

0\leq F(g)=F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p})-F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{q})=-F(f).

Hence,
F(f)=0
.
∎

4
Extending the information loss functor

Proposition
3.11
shows it is not possible to extend the information loss functor to a functor on
\mathbf{FinPS}
. Nevertheless, in this section, we define a non-vanishing
function

K:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
that restricts to the information loss
functor
on
\mathbf{FinPD}
, which we refer to as
conditional information loss
. While
K
is not functorial, we show that it satisfies many important properties such as continuity, convex linearity, and invariance with respect to compositions with isomorphisms. Furthermore, in Section
5
we show
K
is functorial on a restricted class of composable pairs of morphisms (cf. Definition
5.15
), which are definable in any Markov category. At the end of this section we characterize conditional information loss as the unique extension of the information loss functor satisfying the reduction axiom 4 (a) as stated in the introduction. In Section
8
, we prove an intrinsic characterization theorem for
K
without reference to the deterministic subcategory
\mathbf{FinPD}
inside
\mathbf{FinPS}
. Appendix
A
provides an interpretation of the vanishing of conditional information loss in terms of correctable codes.

(X\times Y,\vartheta(f))\xrightarrow{\pi_{Y}}(Y,q)
is the projection and
\vartheta(f)
is the joint distribution (cf. Definition
2.11
).

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

Applying
K
to
f
yields

\begin{split}K(f)&=-\sum_{x,y}f_{yx}p_{x}\log(f_{yx})-\sum_{x}p_{x}\log(p_{x})+\sum_{y}q_{y}\log(q_{y})\\
&=-\sum_{x,y}f_{yx}p_{x}\log(f_{yx})-\sum_{x}\left(\sum_{y}f_{yx}\right)p_{x}\log(p_{x})+\sum_{y}\left(\sum_{x}f_{yx}p_{x}\right)\log(q_{y})\\
&=-\sum_{x\in X\setminus N_{p}}\sum_{y\in Y\setminus N_{f_{x}}}f_{yx}p_{x}\log\left(\frac{f_{yx}p_{x}}{q_{y}}\right).\qed\end{split}

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

you found me!

i)

The non-negativity of
K
follows from Lemma
4.3
and the equality
q_{y}=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in X}f_{yx^{\prime}}p_{x^{\prime}}\geq f_{yx}p_{x}
.

ii)

This follows from the fact that
H(f|p)=0
for all deterministic
f
.

be a sequence (indexed by

) of probability-preserving stochastic maps such that

for large enough

, and where

. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}K\big(f^{(n)}\big)=-\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{x,y}f^{(n)}_{yx}p^{(n)}_{x}\log\left(\frac{f^{(n)}_{yx}p^{(n)}_{x}}{\sum_{x^{\prime}}f^{(n)}_{yx^{\prime}}p^{(n)}_{x^{\prime}}}\right)=K(f),

where the last equality follows from the fact that the limit and sum (which is finite) can be interchanged and all expressions are continuous on
[0,1]
.

v)

This follows from

\begin{split}H\big(\vartheta(f)\big)&=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in X\\
y\in Y\end{subarray}}f_{yx}p_{x}\log(f_{yx}p_{x})=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in X\\
y\in Y\end{subarray}}f_{yx}p_{x}\log(f_{yx})-\underbrace{\sum_{y\in Y}f_{yx}}_{=1}\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}\log(p_{x})\\
&=H(f|p)+H(p)\end{split}

and the fact that
K(\pi_{Y})=H\big(\vartheta(f)\big)-H(q)
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Since conditional entropy vanishes for deterministic morphisms, conditional information loss restricts to
\mathbf{FinPD}
as the information loss functor. It is important to note that if the term
H(f|p)
was not included in the expression for
K(f)
, then the inequality
K(f)\geq 0
would fail in general.
When
f
is deterministic, Baez, Fritz, and Leinster proved
H(p)-H(q)\geq 0
. However, when
f
is stochastic, the inequality
H(p)-H(q)\geq 0
does not hold in general. This has to do with the fact that stochastic maps may increase entropy, whereas deterministic maps always decrease it (in the present classical setting).
6
6

6

This no longer holds for quantum systems
[
11
]
.

As such, the term
H(f|p)
is needed to retain non-negativity as one attempts to extend BFL’s functor
K
on
\mathbf{FinPD}
to a function on
\mathbf{FinPS}
.

Proposition 4.6

(Reductive characterization of conditional information loss)
.

Let
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
be a function satisfying the following conditions.

i)

F
restricted to
\mathbf{FinPD}
is functorial, convex linear, and continuous.

ii)

F
is reductive.

Then
F
is a non-negative multiple of conditional information loss. Conversely, conditional information loss satisfies conditions
i
and
ii
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Theorem
3.10
and item
v
of Proposition
4.2
.
∎

In what follows, we will characterize conditional information loss without any explicit reference to the subcatgeory
\mathbf{FinPD}
or the information loss functor of Baez, Fritz, and Leinster. To do this, we first need to develop some machinery.

5
Coalescable morphisms and semi-functoriality

While conditional information loss is not functorial on
\mathbf{FinPS}
, we know it acts functorially on deterministic maps. As such, it is natural to ask for which pairs of composable stochastic maps does the conditional information loss act functorially. In this section, we answer this question, and then we use our result to define a property of functions
\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
that is a weakening of functoriality, and which we refer to as
semi-functoriality
. Our definitions
are valid in any Markov category (cf. Appendix
B
).

{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.42 -6.28 L 8.59 -6.28 C 7.55 -6.28 7.04 -7.46 6 -7.46 C 5.26 -7.46 4.61 -6.89 3.94 -6.28 C 3.26 -5.68 2.62 -5.1 1.87 -5.1 C 1.12 -5.1 0.48 -5.68 -0.2 -6.28 C -0.87 -6.89 -1.51 -7.46 -2.26 -7.46 C -3.01 -7.46 -3.66 -6.89 -4.33 -6.28 C -5 -5.68 -5.65 -5.1 -6.4 -5.1 L -9.97 -6.28 L -9.97 -6.28}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.0067pt}{-4.54025pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(Z,r)
be a composable pair of morphisms in
\mathbf{FinPS}
.
Then the following statements are equivalent.

(a)

For every
x\in X\setminus N_{p}
and
z\in Z
, there exists
at most
one
y\in Y
such that
g_{zy}f_{yx}\neq 0
.

(b)

The pair
(f,g)
admits a mediator
Z\times X\xrightarrow{h}Y
.

(c)

There exists a function
Z\times X\xrightarrow{h}Y
such that

h_{y(z,x)}(g\circ f)_{zx}p_{x}=g_{yz}f_{yx}p_{x}\qquad\forall\;(z,y,x)\in Z\times Y\times X.

(5.5)

Proof.

you found me!

(
a
\Rightarrow
b
) For every
(z,x)\in Z\times(X\setminus N_{p})
for which such a
y
exists, set
h(z,x):=y
. If no such
y
exists or if
x\in N_{p}
, set
h(z,x)
to be anything. Then
h
is a mediator for
(f,g)
.

(
b
\Rightarrow
c
) Let
h
be a mediator for
(f,g)
. Since (
5.5
) holds automatically for
x\in N_{p}
, suppose
x\in X\setminus N_{p}
, in which case (
5.5
) is equivalent to
h_{y(z,x)}(g\circ f)_{zx}=g_{yz}f_{yx}
for all
(z,y)\in Z\times Y
. This follows from Equation (
5.2
) and the fact that
h
is a function.

(
c
\Rightarrow
a
) Let
(z,x)\in Z\times(X\setminus N_{p})
and suppose
(g\circ f)_{zx}>0
. If
h
is the mediator, then
\sum_{y\in Y}g_{zy}f_{yx}=(g\circ f)_{zx}=g_{zh(z,x)}f_{h(z,x)x}
. But since
g_{zy}f_{yx}=0
for all
y\neq h(z,x)
, there is only one non-vanishing term in this sum, and it is precisely
g_{zh(z,x)}f_{h(z,x)x}
.
∎

Theorem 5.6

(Functoriality of Conditional Entropy)
.

In particular,

H(g\circ f|p)=H(g|q)+H(f|p)
if and only if
H(g\circ f|p)=H\big((g\times\mathrm{id}_{Y})\circ\Delta_{Y}\circ f|p\big)
.

Proof of Lemma
5.8
.

On components,

\big((g\times\mathrm{id}_{Y})\circ\Delta_{Y}\circ f\big)_{(z,y)x}=g_{zy}f_{yx}
.
Hence,

\begin{split}H\big((g\times\mathrm{id}_{Y})\circ\Delta_{Y}\circ f|p\big)&=-\sum_{x}p_{x}\left(\sum_{y,z}g_{zy}f_{yx}\log(g_{zy}f_{yx})\right)\\
&=-\sum_{x,y,z}p_{x}g_{zy}f_{yx}\log(g_{zy})-\sum_{x,y,z}p_{x}g_{zy}f_{yx}\log(f_{yx})\\
&=-\sum_{y,z}q_{y}g_{zy}f_{yx}\log(g_{zy})-\sum_{x,y}p_{x}f_{yx}\log(f_{yx})\\
&=H(g|q)+H(f|p).\end{split}

Note that this equality still holds if
g_{zy}=0
or
f_{yx}=0
as each step in this calculation accounted for such possibilities.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

be a pair of composable morphisms in
\mathbf{FinPS}
. Then

\begin{split}0&\leq H\big((g\times\mathrm{id}_{Y})\circ\Delta_{Y}\circ f|p\big)-H(g\circ f|p)\\
&=-\sum_{x\in X\setminus N_{p}}\sum_{y\in Y\setminus N_{f_{x}}}\sum_{z\in Z\setminus N_{g_{y}}}p_{x}g_{zy}f_{yx}\log\left(\frac{g_{zy}f_{yx}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}}g_{zy^{\prime}}f_{y^{\prime}x}}\right).\end{split}

(5.10)

Note that the order of the sums matters in this expression and also note that it is always well-defined since
g_{zy}f_{yx}\neq 0
implies
(g\circ f)_{zx}\neq 0
.

Proof of Lemma
5.9
.

For convenience, temporarily set
\aleph:=H\big((g\times\mathrm{id}_{Y})\circ\Delta_{Y}\circ f|p\big)-H(g\circ f|p)
. Then

\begin{split}\aleph&=-\sum_{x,y,z}p_{x}g_{zy}f_{yx}\log(g_{zy}f_{yx})+\sum_{x,y,z}p_{x}g_{zy}f_{yx}\log\big((g\circ f)_{zx}\big)\\
&=-\sum_{x\in X\setminus N_{p}}\sum_{y\in Y\setminus N_{f_{x}}}\sum_{z\in Z\setminus N_{g_{y}}}p_{x}g_{zy}f_{yx}\log\left(\frac{g_{zy}f_{yx}}{(g\circ f)_{zx}}\right),\end{split}

which proves the claim due to the definition of the composition of stochastic maps.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.6

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}Z\times X
associated with the composite
g\circ f
and prior
p
, namely

s_{(z,x)}:=(g\circ f)_{zx}p_{x}
.
Then,

\big\{(z,x)\;:\;x\in X\setminus N_{p},\,z\in Z\setminus N_{(g\circ f)_{x}}\big\}=(Z\times X)\setminus N_{s}.

(5.11)

(
\Rightarrow
) Suppose
\aleph=0
, which is equivalent to Equation (
5.7
) by Lemma
5.8
. Then since each term in the sum from Lemma
5.9
is non-negative,

0=-g_{zy}f_{yx}p_{x}\log\left(\frac{g_{zy}f_{yx}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}}g_{zy^{\prime}}f_{y^{\prime}x}}\right)\quad\forall\;x\in X\setminus N_{p},\;y\in Y\setminus N_{f_{x}},\;z\in Z\in N_{g_{y}}.

Hence, fix such an
x\in X\setminus N_{p},y\in Y\setminus N_{f_{x}},z\in Z\in N_{g_{y}}
. The expression here vanishes if and only if

g_{zy}f_{yx}=(g\circ f)_{zx},\quad\text{ i.e.\ }\quad g_{zy^{\prime}}f_{y^{\prime}x}=0\;\;\forall\;y^{\prime}\in Y\setminus\{y\}.

(5.12)

Hence, for every
x\in X\setminus N_{p}
and
z\in Z\setminus N_{(g\circ f)_{x}}
, there exists a unique
y\in Y
such that
g_{zy}f_{yx}\neq 0
.
But by (
5.11
), this means that for every
(z,x)\in(Z\times X)\setminus N_{s}
, there exists a unique
y\in Y
such that
g_{zy}f_{yx}\neq 0
. This defines a function
(Z\times X)\setminus N_{s}\to Y\setminus N_{q}
which can be extended in an
s
-a.e. unique manner to a function
Z\times X\xrightarrow{h}Y.

We now show the function
h
is in fact a mediator for the composable pair
(g,f)
.
The equality clearly holds if
x\in N_{p}
since both sides vanish. Hence, suppose that
x\in X\setminus N_{p}
. Given
y\in Y,z\in Z
, the left-hand-side of (
5.5
) equals

\delta_{yh(z,x)}(g\circ f)_{zx}p_{x}\overset{\text{(\ref{eq:gfhasoneterm})}}{=\joinrel=\joinrel=}\begin{cases}g_{zy}f_{yx}p_{x}&\mbox{ if $z\in Z\setminus N_{(g\circ f)_{x}}$ and $y=h(z,x)$}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

Similarly, if
x\in X\setminus N_{p}
and
z\in N_{(g\circ f)_{x}}
, then
g_{zy}f_{yx}=0
for all
y\in Y
because otherwise
(g\circ f)_{zx}p_{x}
would be nonzero. If instead
z\in Z\setminus N_{(g\circ f)_{x}}
, then
g_{zh(z,x)}f_{h(z,x)x}\neq 0
and
g_{zy}f_{yx}=0
for all
y\in Y\setminus\{h(z,x)\}
by (
5.12
). Therefore, (
5.5
) holds.

(
\Leftarrow
) Conversely, suppose a mediator
h
exists and let
X\mathrel{\hbox to15.08pt{\vbox to11.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.54056pt\lower-7.03331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.9878pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{}{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.6 -6.62 L 8.77 -6.62 C 7.74 -6.62 7.22 -7.8 6.19 -7.8 C 5.44 -7.8 4.8 -7.22 4.12 -6.62 C 3.45 -6.01 2.8 -5.44 2.06 -5.44 C 1.31 -5.44 0.66 -6.01 -0.01 -6.62 C -0.68 -7.22 -1.33 -7.8 -2.08 -7.8 C -2.83 -7.8 -3.47 -7.22 -4.14 -6.62 C -4.82 -6.01 -5.46 -5.44 -6.21 -5.44 L -10.16 -6.62 L -10.16 -6.62}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.14056pt}{-4.78331pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}Z\times Y
be the stochastic map given on components by

k_{(z,y)x}:=h_{y(z,x)}(g\circ f)_{zx}.

Then

\begin{split}H(g|q)+H(f|p)&=H\big((g\times\mathrm{id}_{Y})\circ\Delta_{Y}\circ f|p\big)\quad\text{ by Lemma~\ref{lem:conditionalmemoryadds}}\\
&=H(k|p)\quad\hskip 82.51299pt\text{ by Proposition~\ref{prop:mediatorequivalence} item~(c)}\\
&=-\sum_{x\in X\setminus N_{p}}\sum_{z\in Z\setminus N_{(g\circ f)_{x}}}\sum_{y\in Y}h_{y(z,x)}(g\circ f)_{zx}p_{x}\log\big(h_{y(z,x)}(g\circ f)_{zx}\big)\\
&=-\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{(z,x)\in(Z\times X)\setminus N_{s}}\sum_{y\in Y}\delta_{yh(z,x)}(g\circ f)_{zx}p_{x}\log\big(\delta_{yh(z,x)}(g\circ f)_{zx}\big)\\
&=-\!\!\!\!\sum_{(z,x)\in(Z\times X)\setminus N_{s}}(g\circ f)_{zx}p_{x}\log\big((g\circ f)_{zx}\big)\\
&=H\big((g\circ f)|p\big),\end{split}

as desired.
∎

.

Proof.

Since the Shannon entropy difference is always functorial, the conditional information loss is functorial on a pair of morphisms if and only if the conditional entropy is functorial on that pair. Theorem
5.6
then completes the proof.
∎

Example 5.14
.

In the notation of Theorem
5.6
, suppose that
f
is
a.e. deterministic
, which means
f_{yx}=\delta_{yf(x)}
for all
x\in X\setminus N_{p}
for some function
f
(abusive notation is used). In this case, the deviation from functoriality, (
5.10
), simplifies to

H\big((g\times\mathrm{id}_{Y})\circ\Delta_{Y}\circ f|p\big)-H(g\circ f|p)=-\sum_{x\in X\setminus N_{p}}\sum_{z\in Z}p_{x}g_{zf(x)}\log\left(\frac{g_{zf(x)}}{g_{zf(x)}}\right)=0.

Therefore, if
f
is
p
-a.e. deterministic,
H(g|q)+H(f|p)=H(g\circ f|p)
. In this case, the mediator
Z\times X\xrightarrow{h}Y
is given by
h:=!_{Z}\times f
.

is a.e. coalescable for any

is coalescable.

In light of Theorem
5.6
and Corollary
5.13
, we make the following definition, which will serve as one of the axioms in our later characterizions of both conditional information loss and conditional entropy.

Definition 5.17
.

A function
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
is said to be
semi-functorial
iff
F(g\circ f)=F(g)+F(f)
for every a.e. coalescable pair
(X,p)\mathrel{\hbox to15.35pt{\vbox to12.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.67252pt\lower-7.71387pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

in
\mathbf{FinPS}
.

Example 5.18
.

By Theorem
5.6
and Corollary
5.13
, conditional information loss and conditional entropy are both semi-functorial.

Proposition 5.19
.

Suppose
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
is semi-functorial. Then the restriction of
F
to
\mathbf{FinPD}
is functorial.
In particular, if
F
is, in addition, convex linear, continuous, and reductive, then
F
is a non-negative multiple of conditional information loss.

Proof.

By Example
5.14
, every pair of composable morphisms in
\mathbf{FinPD}
is a.e. coalescable. Therefore,
F
is functorial on
\mathbf{FinPD}
. The second claim then follows from Proposition
4.6
.
∎

The following lemma will be used in later sections and serves to illustrate some examples of a.e. coalescable pairs.

is coalescable, note that since
g
is an isomorphism we have
(g\circ f)_{zx}=g_{zg^{-1}(z)}f_{g^{-1}(z)x}
.
Thus,
Z\times X\xrightarrow{g^{-1}\times!_{X}}Y\times\bullet\cong Y
is a mediator function for
g\circ f
, thus
g\circ f
is coalescable. The last two claims follow from the proofs of the first two claims.
∎

iv)

Let
(f,g)
be a composable pair of morphisms in
\mathbf{FinPS}
, and suppose
\overline{f}
and
\overline{g}
are Bayesian inverses of
f
and
g
respectively. Then
(\overline{g},\overline{f})
is a composable pair, and
\overline{f}\circ\overline{g}
is a Bayesian inverse of
g\circ f
.

Proof.

These are immediate consequences of the categorical definition of a Bayesian inverse (see
[
6
,
14
,
12
]
for proofs).
∎

Definition 6.5
.

A contravariant function
\mathcal{B}:\mathbf{FinPS}\to\mathbf{FinPS}
is said to be a
Bayesian inversion functor
7
7

7

This is mildly abusive terminology since functoriality only holds in the a.e. sense (cf. Remark
6.6
).

if and only if
\mathcal{B}
acts as the identity on objects and
\mathcal{B}(f)
is a Bayesian inverse of
f
for all morphisms
f
in
\mathbf{FinPS}
.

to be given by
\overline{f}_{xy}=p_{x}f_{yx}/q_{y}
for all

with
q_{y}\neq 0
and
\overline{f}_{xy}=1/|X|
for all
y\in Y
with
q_{y}=0
. Note that this does
not
define a functor. Indeed, if
(X,p)
is a probability space with
p_{x_{0}}=0
for some
x_{0}\in X
, then
(\overline{\mathrm{id}_{X}})_{x_{0}}
is the uniform measure on
X
instead of the Dirac delta measure concentrated on
x_{0}
. In other words,
\overline{\mathrm{id}_{X}}\neq\mathrm{id}_{X}
. Similar issues of measure zero occur, indicating that
\overline{g\circ f}\neq\overline{f}\circ\overline{g}
for a composable pair of morphisms
(X,p)\mathrel{\hbox to15.35pt{\vbox to12.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.67252pt\lower-7.71387pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a Bayesian inversion functor on
\mathbf{FinPS}
(as in Definition
6.5
). Then
\mathcal{B}
is
a.e. convex linear
in the sense that

\mathcal{B}\left(\bigoplus_{x\in X}p_{x}Q^{x}\right)\underset{\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize${q^{\prime}}$}}{=}\bigoplus_{x\in X}p_{x}\mathcal{B}(Q^{x}),

where
q^{\prime}:=\bigoplus_{x\in X}p_{x}q^{\prime x}
and the other notation is as in Definition
2.12
.

Proof.

First note that it is immediate that
\mathcal{B}
is convex linear on objects since Bayesian inversion acts as the identity on objects.
Let
p:\bullet\mathrel{\hbox to10.66pt{\vbox to6.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.33047pt\lower-4.60277pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(Y^{\prime}_{x},q^{\prime x})
be a collection of morphisms in
\mathbf{FinPS}
indexed by
X
, and suppose
\mathcal{B}
is a Bayesian inversion functor. Then for
(z,z^{\prime})\in Y_{x}\times Y^{\prime}_{x}
with
p_{x}q^{\prime x}_{z^{\prime}}\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\left(\bigoplus_{x\in X}p_{x}Q^{x}\right)_{zz^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{x}q^{x}_{z}\left(\bigoplus_{x\in X}p_{x}Q^{x}\right)_{z^{\prime}z}}{p_{x}q^{\prime x}_{z^{\prime}}}=\frac{q^{x}_{z}\left(Q^{x}_{z^{\prime}z}\right)}{q^{\prime x}_{z^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(Q^{x})_{zz^{\prime}}=\left(\bigoplus_{x\in X}p_{x}\mathcal{B}(Q^{x})\right)_{zz^{\prime}}.

Thus,
\mathcal{B}
is a.e. convex linear.
∎

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(Z,r)
in
\mathbf{FinPS}
, and let
\overline{f}
and
\overline{g}
be Bayesian inverses of
f
and
g
respectively. Then
(f,g)
is a.e. coalescable if and only if
(\overline{g},\overline{f})
is a.e. coalescable.

Proof.

Since Bayesian inversion is a dagger functor on a.e. equivalence classes
[
6
, Remark 13.9]
, it suffices to prove one direction in this claim. Hence, suppose
(f,g)
is a.e. coalescable and let
h
be a mediator function realizing this. Then
h\circ\gamma
is a mediator for
(\overline{g},\overline{f})
because

\begin{split}\overline{f}_{xy}\overline{g}_{yz}r_{z}&=\overline{f}_{xy}g_{zy}q_{y}=g_{zy}f_{yx}p_{x}=h_{y(z,x)}(g\circ f)_{zx}p_{x}\\
&=h_{y(z,x)}\overline{(g\circ f)}_{xz}r_{z}=(h\circ\gamma)_{y(x,z)}\overline{(g\circ f)}_{xz}r_{z}.\end{split}

A completely string-diagrammatic proof is provided in Appendix
B
.
∎

The following proposition is a reformulation of the conditional entropy identity
H(\mathcal{Y}|\mathcal{X})+H(\mathcal{X})=H(\mathcal{X}|\mathcal{Y})+H(\mathcal{Y})
in terms of Bayesian inversion.

Proposition
6.10
implies Bayesian inversion takes conditional entropy to conditional information loss and vice versa, which is formally stated as follows.

Corollary 6.12
.

Let
K:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
and
H:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
be given by conditional information loss and conditional entropy, respectively, and let
\mathcal{B}:\mathbf{FinPS}\to\mathbf{FinPS}
be a Bayesian inversion functor. Then
8
8

8

Since contravariant functions into
{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
are covariant and vice versa (cf. Convention
3.2
), the composites
K\circ\mathcal{B}
and
H\circ\mathcal{B}
are all covariant, which is why we have not written any
op
superscripts.

H=K\circ\mathcal{B}
and
K=H\circ\mathcal{B}
.

Remark 6.13
.

If
(X,p)\overset{f}{\to}(Y,q)
is a deterministic morphism in
\mathbf{FinPS}
, Baez, Fritz, and Leinster point out that the information loss of
f
is in fact the conditional entropy of
x
given
y

[
1
]
.
Here, we see this duality as a special case of Corollary
6.12
applied to deterministic morphisms.

7
Bloom-shriek factorization

We now introduce a simple, but surprisingly useful, factorization for every morphism in
\mathbf{FinPS}
, and we use it to prove some essential lemmas for our characterization theorems for conditional information loss and conditional entropy, which appear in the following sections.

Definition 7.3
.

The decomposition in item
ii
Proposition
7.2
will be referred to as the
bloom-shriek factorization
of
f
.

Proof of Propostion
7.2
.

Element-wise proofs are left as exercises. Appendix
B
contains an abstract proof using string diagrams in Markov categories.
∎

The bloom of
f
can be expressed as a convex combination of simpler morphisms up to isomorphism. To describe this and its behavior under convex linear semi-functors, we introduce the notion of an invariant and examine some of its properties.

Lemma 7.5
.

If a function
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
is semi-functorial, then
F
is an invariant.

Proof.

Consider a composable triple
(W,s)\xrightarrow{e}(X,p)\mathrel{\hbox to15.35pt{\vbox to12.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.67252pt\lower-7.71387pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.11975pt}{-1.75pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{}{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.79 -7.56 L 8.96 -7.56 C 7.92 -7.56 7.41 -8.74 6.37 -8.74 C 5.62 -8.74 4.98 -8.17 4.31 -7.56 C 3.63 -6.96 2.99 -6.38 2.24 -6.38 C 1.49 -6.38 0.85 -6.96 0.17 -7.56 C -0.5 -8.17 -1.15 -8.74 -1.9 -8.74 C -2.64 -8.74 -3.29 -8.17 -3.96 -7.56 C -4.64 -6.96 -5.28 -6.38 -6.03 -6.38 L -10.34 -7.56 L -10.34 -7.56}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.27252pt}{-5.46387pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{

such that
e
and
g
are isomorphisms. Then

F\big(g\circ f\circ e\big)=F\big(g\circ(f\circ e)\big)=F(g)+F(f\circ e)=F(g)+F(f)+F(e)

by Lemma
5.20
. Secondly, since
g
and
e
are isomorphisms, and since the pairs
(g,g^{-1})
and
(e,e^{-1})
are coalescable,
F(\mathrm{id})=F(g\circ g^{-1})=F(g)+F(g^{-1})
. But since
F(\mathrm{id})=0
(by semi-functoriality), this requires that
F(g)=0
for an isomorphism
g
since
F(g)\geq 0
and
F(g^{-1})\geq 0
. The same is true for
F(e)
. Hence,
F\big(g\circ f\circ e\big)=F(f)
.
∎

{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.79 -7.56 L 8.96 -7.56 C 7.92 -7.56 7.41 -8.74 6.37 -8.74 C 5.62 -8.74 4.98 -8.17 4.31 -7.56 C 3.63 -6.96 2.99 -6.38 2.24 -6.38 C 1.49 -6.38 0.85 -6.96 0.17 -7.56 C -0.5 -8.17 -1.15 -8.74 -1.9 -8.74 C -2.64 -8.74 -3.29 -8.17 -3.96 -7.56 C -4.64 -6.96 -5.28 -6.38 -6.03 -6.38 L -10.34 -7.56 L -10.34 -7.56}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.27252pt}{-5.46387pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(Y,q)
be a morphism in
\mathbf{FinPS}
, and suppose
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
is semi-functorial and convex linear. Then the following statements hold.

i)

F(f)=F(\operatorname{!}_{\overline{f}})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f})

ii)

F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f})=\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f_{x}})

iii)

F(\operatorname{!}_{f})=\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}F(\operatorname{!}_{f_{x}})

Proof.

For item
i
, we have

\displaystyle F(f)

\displaystyle=F(!_{\overline{f}}\circ\gamma\circ\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f})

by item
ii
of Proposition
7.2

\displaystyle=F(!_{\overline{f}}\circ\gamma)+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f})

by item
iii
of Proposition
7.2

\displaystyle=F(\operatorname{!}_{\overline{f}})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f})

\displaystyle\text{ by Lemma~\ref{lem:Fsemifunctinvariant}}.

For items
ii
and
iii
, note that
\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f}
and
\operatorname{!}_{f}
can be expressed as composites of isomorphisms and certain convex combinations, namely

(7.7)

Hence,

\displaystyle F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f})

\displaystyle=F(\varphi\circ\ell\circ\psi)

\displaystyle F(\operatorname{!}_{f})

\displaystyle=F(\varphi^{\prime}\circ\ell^{\prime}\circ\psi^{\prime})

by (
7.7
)

\displaystyle=F(\ell)

\displaystyle=F(\ell^{\prime})

by Lemma
7.5

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f_{x}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}F(\operatorname{!}_{f_{x}})

\displaystyle\text{ since $F$ is convex linear.}\qed

Proposition 7.8
.

F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
is semi-functorial and convex linear.
If
f,g:(X,p)\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.07996pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!(Y,q)
are two morphisms in
\mathbf{FinPS}
such that

, then

.

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(Y,q)
are such that
f\underset{\raisebox{1.80835pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize$p$}}{=}g
, and let
\overline{f}
and
\overline{g}
be Bayesian inverses for
f
and
g
.
Then

\displaystyle F(f)

\displaystyle=F(\operatorname{!}_{\overline{f}})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f})

by item
i
of Lemma
7.6

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Y}q_{y}F(\operatorname{!}_{\overline{f}_{y}})+\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f_{x}})

by items
ii
and
iii
of Lemma
7.6

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in Y}q_{y}F(\operatorname{!}_{\overline{g}_{y}})+\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{g_{x}})

since
f\underset{\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize${p}$}}{=}g
and
\overline{f}\underset{\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize${q}$}}{=}\overline{g}

\displaystyle=F(\operatorname{!}_{\overline{g}})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{g})

by items
ii
and
iii
of Lemma
7.6

\displaystyle=F(g)

by item
i
of Lemma
7.6
,

as desired.
∎

8
An intrinsic characterization of conditional information loss

Theorem 8.1
.

Suppose
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
is a function satisfying the following conditions.

1.

F
is semi-functorial.

2.

F
is convex linear.

3.

F
is continuous.

4.

F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p})=0
for every probability distribution
\bullet\mathrel{\hbox to14.78pt{\vbox to11.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.39165pt\lower-6.79025pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

.

, let

be an arbitrary morphism in
\mathbf{FinPS}
, and let
\gamma:Y\times X\to X\times Y
be the swap map, so that
\operatorname{!}_{\overline{f}}=\pi_{Y}\circ\gamma
. Then

\displaystyle F(f)

\displaystyle=F(\operatorname{!}_{\overline{f}})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f})

by item
i
of Lemma
7.6

\displaystyle=F(\operatorname{!}_{\overline{f}})+\sum_{x\in X}p_{x}F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f_{x}})

by item
ii
of Lemma
7.6

\displaystyle=F(\operatorname{!}_{\overline{f}})

by condition
4

\displaystyle=F\left(\pi_{Y}\circ\gamma\right)

\displaystyle=F(\pi_{Y})

since
F
is an invariant (Definition
7.4
).

Thus,
F
is reductive (see Definition
4.5
)
and Proposition
5.19
applies.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

Under the assumption that
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
is semi-functorial and convex linear, one may show
F
satisfies condition
4
in Theorem
8.1
if and only if
F
is reductive (see Definition
4.5
and Proposition
5.19
).
While the reductive axiom specifies how the semi-functor acts on
all
morphisms in
\mathbf{FinPS}
, condition
4
in Theorem
8.1
only specifies how it acts on morphisms from the
initial
object. This gives not just a simple mathematical criterion, but one with a simple intuitive interpretation as well.
Namely, condition
4
says that if a process begins with no prior information, then there is no information to be lost in the process.

We now use Theorem
8.1
and Bayesian inversion to prove a statement dual to Theorem
8.1
.

Theorem 8.3
.

Suppose
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
is a function satisfying the following conditions.

is semi-functorial.

2.

is convex linear.

F
is continuous.

for every probability distribution

Then

is a non-negative multiple of conditional entropy. Conversely, conditional entropy satisfies conditions
1
-
4
.

Before giving a proof, we introduce some terminology and prove a few lemmas. We also would like to point out that condition
4
may be given an operational interpretation as follows: if a communication channel has a constant output, then it has no conditional entropy.

Definition 8.4
.

Let
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
be a function and let
\mathcal{B}
be a Bayesian inversion functor. Then
\overline{F}:=F\circ\mathcal{B}
will be referred to as a
Bayesian reflection
of
F
.

Remark 8.5
.

By Proposition
7.8
, if
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
is a convex linear semi-functor, then a Bayesian reflection is independent of the choice of a Bayesian inversion functor, and as such, is necessarily unique.

\overline{F}(f)=F(\overline{f})
.

Proof of Lemma
8.6
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a Bayesian inversion functor, so that
\mathcal{B}(f)\underset{\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize${q}$}}{=}\overline{f}
. Then

\overline{F}(f)=F(\mathcal{B}(f))=F(\overline{f}),

where the last equality follows from Proposition
7.8
.
∎

. Then
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{B}(f_{n})\underset{\raisebox{0.90417pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\scriptsize${q}$}}{=}\mathcal{B}(f)
.

Proof of Lemma
8.7
.

Set
f^{(n)}:=f_{n}
.
For all
y\in Y
with
q_{y}\neq 0
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{B}\big(f^{(n)}\big)_{xy}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{p^{(n)}_{x}f^{(n)}_{yx}}{q^{(n)}_{y}}=\frac{p_{x}f_{yx}}{q_{y}}=\mathcal{B}(f)_{xy}.\qed

Lemma 8.8
.

Suppose
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
is a function satisfying conditions
1
-
4
of Theorem
8.3
. Then the Bayesian reflection
\overline{F}
is a non-negative multiple of conditional information loss.

Proof of Lemma
8.8
.

We show
\overline{F}
satisfies conditions
1
-
4
of Theorem
8.1
. Throughout the proof, let
\mathcal{B}
denote a Bayesian inversion functor, so that
\overline{F}=F\circ\mathcal{B}
.

.

is semi-functorial.

: This follows from Lemma

, since
\operatorname{!}_{p}
is the unique Bayesian inverse of

Proof of Theorem
8.3
.

Suppose
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
is a function satisfying conditions
1
-
4
of Theorem
8.3
, and let
\mathcal{B}
be a Bayesian inversion functor. Since
F
is semi-functorial and convex linear it follows from Proposition
7.8
that
F=\overline{F}\circ\mathcal{B}
, and by Lemma
8.8
it follows that
\overline{F}=cK
for some non-negative constant
c\geq 0
. We then have
F=\overline{F}\circ\mathcal{B}=cK\circ\mathcal{B}=cH
, thus
F
is a non-negative multiple of conditional entropy.
∎

9
A Bayesian characterization of conditional entropy

We now prove a reformulation of Theorem
8.3
where condition
4
is replaced by a condition which we view as an ‘entropic Bayes’ rule’.

Definition 9.1
.

A function
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
satisfies an
entropic Bayes’ rule
if and only if

F(f)+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p})=F(\overline{f})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{q})

for every morphism
(X,p)\mathrel{\hbox to15.35pt{\vbox to12.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.67252pt\lower-7.71387pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Remark 9.2
.

The entropic Bayes’ rule is an abstraction of the conditional entropy identity (
6.11
).

Theorem 9.3

(A Bayesian characterization of conditional entropy)
.

Suppose
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\geq 0}
is a function satisfying the following conditions.

1.

F
is semi-functorial.

2.

F
is convex linear.

3.

F
is continuous.

4.

F
satisfies an entropic Bayes’ rule.

Then
F
is a non-negative multiple of conditional entropy. Conversely, conditional entropy satisfies conditions
1
-
4
.

Proof.

By Theorem
8.3
, it suffices to show
F(\operatorname{!}_{p})=0
for every object
(X,p)
in
\mathbf{FinPS}
.
For this, first note that
\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1}=\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1}\circ\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1}
, where
(\bullet,1)
is the point-mass distribution on a single point. Since
F
is semi-functorial and
\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1}\circ\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1}
is coalescable, we have

F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1})=F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1})
, which implies
F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1})=0
.
Applying the entropic Bayes’ rule from Definition
9.1

to the morphism
\operatorname{!}_{p}:(X,p)\mathrel{\hbox to10.66pt{\vbox to6.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.33047pt\lower-4.60277pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.77771pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{}{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}
{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.55 -3.26 L 5.72 -3.26 C 4.68 -3.26 4.17 -4.44 3.13 -4.44 C 2.38 -4.44 1.74 -3.86 1.06 -3.26 C 0.39 -2.65 -0.25 -2.07 -1 -2.07 L -7.1 -3.26 L -7.1 -3.26}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{4.93048pt}{-2.35277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

yields

F(\operatorname{!}_{p})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p})=F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1})\implies F(\operatorname{!}_{p})=F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{1})=0,

as desired.
∎

Remark 9.4
.

In
[
7
, slide 21]
, Fritz asked if there is a Markov category for information theory explaining the analogy between the Bayes rule
\mathbb{P}(A|B)\mathbb{P}(B)=\mathbb{P}(B|A)\mathbb{P}(A)
and the conditional entropy identity
H(A|B)+H(B)=H(B|A)+H(A)
. In light of our work, we feel we have an adequate categorical explanation for this analogy, which we now explain.

Let
(X,p)\mathrel{\hbox to15.35pt{\vbox to12.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.67252pt\lower-7.71387pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.11975pt}{-1.75pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{}{}{}{}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{}{}{}}

}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.79 -7.56 L 8.96 -7.56 C 7.92 -7.56 7.41 -8.74 6.37 -8.74 C 5.62 -8.74 4.98 -8.17 4.31 -7.56 C 3.63 -6.96 2.99 -6.38 2.24 -6.38 C 1.49 -6.38 0.85 -6.96 0.17 -7.56 C -0.5 -8.17 -1.15 -8.74 -1.9 -8.74 C -2.64 -8.74 -3.29 -8.17 -3.96 -7.56 C -4.64 -6.96 -5.28 -6.38 -6.03 -6.38 L -10.34 -7.56 L -10.34 -7.56}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.27252pt}{-5.46387pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.6 2.84 C -2.94 1.13 -1.48 0.33 0 0 C -1.48 -0.33 -2.94 -1.13 -3.6 -2.84}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(Y,q)
be an arbitrary morphism in
\mathbf{FinPS}
, and suppose
F:\mathbf{FinPS}\to{{\mathbb{B}}}{{\mathbb{R}}}
is semi-functorial. Then the commutative diagram (cf. Definition
B.8
)

\textstyle{(\bullet,1)}
\textstyle{(X,p)}
\textstyle{(X\times Y,\vartheta(f))}
\textstyle{(Y,q)}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma\circ\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{\overline{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f}}

(9.5)

is a coalescable square (where
\gamma
is the swap map), i.e.,
\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f}\circ\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p}
and
(\gamma\circ\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{\overline{f}})\circ\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{q}
are both coalescable. The semi-functoriality of
F
then implies the identity
F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p})=F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{\overline{f}})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{q})
. Now suppose—as in the case of conditional entropy—that
F
satisfies the further condition that
F(f)=F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{f})
. Then commutivity of (
9.5
) and this
are equivalent to the following two respective equations:

Bayes’ Rule

\displaystyle:\quad

\displaystyle f_{yx}p_{x}

\displaystyle=\overline{f}_{xy}q_{y}

\displaystyle\underline{\text{\smash{Entropic Bayes' Rule}}}

\displaystyle:\quad

\displaystyle F(f)+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{p})

\displaystyle=F(\overline{f})+F(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{q}).

In the case that
F=H
, where
H
is the conditional entropy, we have
H(\operatorname{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$!$}}_{r}|1)=H(r)
for every object
(Z,r)
in
\mathbf{FinPS}
(where
H(r)
is the Shannon entropy). Thus, the entropic Bayes’ rule becomes
H(f|p)+H(p)=H(\overline{f}|q)+H(q)
, which is the classical identity for conditional entropy.

Lemma A.2
.

If
f
is a disintegration of
g
, then
g
is
q
-
a.e.
deterministic and
f
is a Bayesian inverse of
g
. Conversely, if
g
is
q
-a.e. deterministic, then every Bayesian inverse of
g
is a disintegration of
g
.

Proof.

This is proved in a more abstract setting in
[
12
, Section 8]
.
∎

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(X,p)
such that
f
is a disintegration of
g
.

Proof.

The theorem will be proved by showing the equivalent statement ‘
H(\overline{f}|q)=0
if and only if
\overline{f}
is
q
-a.e. deterministic for some Bayesian inverse
\overline{f}
of
f
.’ We therefore first justify this as being equivalent to the claim.

First,
K(f)=0
holds if and only if
H(\overline{f})=0
for some (and hence any) Bayesian inverse
\overline{f}
of
f
by Corollary
6.12
and Lemma
8.6
. Second, the statement ‘there exists a
g
such that
f
is a disintegration of
g
’ holds if and only if ‘there exists a
g
such that
f
is a Bayesian inverse of
g

and

g
is
q
-a.e. deterministic’ by Lemma
A.2
. However, since Bayesian inverses always exist (Theorem
6.2
), and because Bayesian inversion is symmetric (item
ii
in Proposition
6.4
), this latter statement is equivalent to ‘there exists a
q
-a.e. deterministic Bayesian inverse
g
of
f
.’

Hence, suppose
f
has a
q
-a.e. deterministic Bayesian inverse
g
. Then

H(g|q)=\sum_{y\in Y}q_{y}H(g_{y})=\sum_{y\in Y\setminus N_{q}}q_{y}H(g_{y})=0,

since the entropy of
g_{y}
vanishes because it is
\{0,1\}
-valued for all
y\in Y\setminus N_{q}
.

Conversely, suppose
H(\overline{f})=0
for some Bayesian inverse
\overline{f}
of
f
. Then
H(\overline{f})=\sum_{y\in Y\setminus N_{q}}q_{y}H(\overline{f}_{y})
is a sum of non-negative numbers that vanishes. Hence,
H(\overline{f}_{y})=0
for all
y\in Y\setminus N_{q}
. But since the entropy of a probability measure on a finite set vanishes if and only if the probability measure is
\{0,1\}
-valued,
\overline{f}_{y}
is
\{0,1\}
-valued for all
y\in Y\setminus N_{q}
. Hence,
\overline{f}
is
q
-a.e. deterministic.
∎

Remark A.4

(Vanishing of Conditional Information Loss in Terms of Correctable Codes)
.

The vanishing of the conditional information loss is closely related to the correctability of classical codes.
9
9

9

Our references for correctable codes include
[
2
,
9
]
, though our particular emphasis in terms of possibilistic maps instead of stochastic maps appears to be new. The correctability of classical codes does not require the datum of a stochastic map, but rather that of a possibilistic map.

A
possibilistic map
(also called a
full relation
) from a finite set
X
to a finite set
Y
is an assignment
f
sending
x\in X
to a nonempty subset
f_{x}\subseteq Y
. Such a map can also be viewed as a transition kernel

as a transition kernel, or equivalently

\lceil f\rceil_{x}:=\big\{y\in Y\;:\;f_{yx}>0\big\}

as a full relation.

(g,q,p)
.

This gives a physical interpretation to the vanishing of conditional information loss. Namely,
K(f)=0
if and only if
(A:=X\setminus N_{p},X,Y,E:=\mathrm{incl},N:=\lceil f\rceil)
is correctable.

Appendix B
The Markov category setting

In this appendix, we gather some definitions and results that indicate how our formalism extends to the setting of Markov categories
[
3
,
6
]
in terms of string diagrams
[
13
]
.

Definition B.1
.

A
Markov category
is a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{M},\otimes,I)
, with
\otimes
the tensor product and
I
the unit (associators and unitors are excluded from the notation), and where each object

In our case,
F=(g\times\mathrm{id}_{Y})\circ\Delta_{Y}\circ f
and
h
is the mediator.
Therefore, a mediator is a deterministic (or at least a.e. deterministic) a.e. conditional for a specific morphism constructed from a pair of composable morphisms.
